# Behavior spec

vidcut copies the behavior of DaVinci Resolve by default. This document describes that behavior in our own words.

It gives the page numbers of the *DaVinci Resolve 21.1 Reference Manual*. The printed page numbers are equal to the PDF page numbers.

[deviations.md](deviations.md) lists where vidcut is different. §12 decides the points that the manual leaves open. §13 gives the order in which a picture is processed.

**Reading the manual:**
- Extract the text of pages with `pdftotext -f <first page> -l <last page> <manual>.pdf -`.
- The developer's copy is in `$VIDCUT_TEST_DATA` (default `~/Downloads/vidcut-tmp`).
- Do not copy its text into the repository.

**Legend:**
- **(later)** is not built yet, and comes after the work in progress.
- A page number, for example (p. 774), tells where the manual gives the rule.
- A rule with no page number and no mark comes from a measurement in Resolve Studio.
- **(not measured)** marks a rule of vidcut where the manual and the measurements give none.
- A line that starts with "vidcut:" tells what vidcut does.
- [todo/gaps.md](todo/gaps.md) lists the Resolve behavior that vidcut does not have.

## 1. Scope

**In scope:**
- media pool, viewer, dual timeline
- editing and trimming, speed changes and speed keyframes (D48)
- transitions: Cross Dissolve, Dip to Color, Edge Wipe, Smooth Cut, Push and Slide, fades to and from black, audio cross fades (§8.11)
- clip/track audio levels and waveforms
- the Equalizer and the Dialogue Leveler of a clip (§9)
- volume keyframes of a clip (§9, D81)
- keyframes of Transform, Crop and Composite (§8.15, D75)
- Voice Isolation, on a clip or a track (§9)
- ducking, the Ducker Track FX (§9)
- subtitle tracks: cues, subtitle files in and out, Create Subtitles from Audio (§8.12)
- titles: Text, the three lower thirds and Scroll (§8.14)
- a clip's Transform, Crop, Dynamic Zoom, composite mode and opacity (§8.15)
- Auto Color, with neural network by default and Use Legacy Auto Color preference (§8.25)
- the Magic Mask of a corrector: a mask of a person or a thing from clicks on one frame (§8.29)
- stabilization of a clip of a video file (§8.27)
- Smart Reframe of a clip of a video file (§8.30)
- video effects: the four groups of the Effects tab, with keyframes of their values (§8.16)
- generators: the twelve kinds of the Generators section (§8.17)
- compound clips (§8.18)
- adjustment clips (§8.24)
- compositions of nodes on the Effects page: Blur, the six effects, and Ellipse, Rectangle and Polygon masks (§8.19)
- the input LUT of a media item and of the project, and the output LUT, from a `.cube` file ([§13](#the-lut-tables))
- the Video Monitor LUT, the Color Viewer LUT and the Scopes LUT ([§13](#the-lut-tables))
- Quick Export (§10) and the Export page (§10.1)
- the Audio page: the rows of the audio tracks, their headers, the meters and the Mixer (§9.1)

**Later (later):**
- other effects, other transitions, other titles, other generators
- keyframes other than Transform, Crop, Composite, speed, the clip volume, effect values and the Position, Zoom, Rotation and Opacity of a title
- the Fusion tools that §8.19 does not have
- LUT files other than `.cube` ([todo](todo/luts.md))
- Lens Correction ([todo](todo/lens-correction.md))
- angle switches ([todo](todo/sync-bin.md)), Live Overwrite
- Replay, voiceover
- AI tools other than Create Subtitles from Audio and Voice Isolation: the other voice and dialogue tools
- Mixer FX other than the Ducker, guides
- interchange formats: DRT, AAF, XML

## 2. Layout (pp. 636–637)

- **Main areas:**
  - The Media Pool is on the left. A single Viewer is on the right.
  - The timeline area is across the bottom.
  - You can move the seam between them, and change the timeline height.
- **Top bar:**
  - On the left: the Media Pool and Mixer tabs (p. 649).
    - A click on the Media Pool tab while the pool shows hides the pool. The Viewer takes its room (p. 636).
    - With the pool hidden, the tabs are in a row above the Viewer. A click on a tab shows what that tab holds.
    - vidcut: the edit-function buttons go with the pool, as they do for the other tabs (not measured).
    - `gui.close media` hides the pool. `gui.show media` shows it.
  - On the right: Quick Export and the Full Screen viewer.
    - The viewer covers the top bar while it fills the page or the display. Then the viewer's header shows Full Screen.
  - A Transitions tab shows the transitions in the place of the Media Pool (p. 759, §8.11).
  - A Titles tab shows the title kinds in the place of the Media Pool (p. 762).
  - An Effects tab shows the video effects in the place of the Media Pool (p. 765, §8.16).
  - The Effects tab also has a Generators page (p. 766, §8.17).
  - The Inspector button is at the top right of the UI toolbar (§8.15). vidcut has an Inspector tab beside the Titles tab instead (deviation X70).
  - A Keyframes tab sits beside the Inspector.
  - The Timeline menu opens that tab.
  - `gui.show keyframes` opens that tab.
  - Resolve opens the editor in a window of its own only on the Edit page (p. 1260).
  - The tab shows the item selected first, a clip or a subtitle.
  - When that item leaves the selection, the tab shows the next item selected, not the earliest on the timeline. Clips and subtitles share this order.
  - **Tracks** (pp. 1255–1256):
    - Each value that can have keyframes has a track, in a group for its section.
    - Volume is a track. Position is one track. Zoom is one track. The anchor point is one track.
    - Volume and Pan are tracks in an Audio group.
    - Speed is one track, in a Speed group. It shows the speed keyframes of the clip (D48, [X94](deviations.md#x94-speed-keyframes-and-the-mark-menus-keyframe-items)).
    - The ⋯ menu has Display All Parameters and Display Parameters with Keyframes. All is the default.
    - A click on the name of a group opens or closes it. The ⋯ menu also has Expand All Parameters and Collapse All Parameters.
  - **Track controls** (p. 1257):
    - The curve button shows the curve of the value under its track.
    - A small dot before the name has the color of the value's line in the graph.
  - **Graph** (p. 1261):
    - The Graph button in the header shows one large graph in the place of the tracks. The names of the tracks stay on its left.
    - The graph shows a colored line for each value with keyframes. The curve button of a track takes its line out or puts it back.
    - The active curve has the points and the handles, and each edit in the graph acts on it. The other curves are dimmed.
    - At first, no curve is active. After a click on an empty part of the graph, no curve is active and no track is selected.
    - A click on a dimmed curve makes it the active curve and clears the selection.
    - A click on a track's name also makes that curve active. The selection stays.
    - The line of a value has the color of its track (p. 1261).
    - Under the graph, a row shows the keyframes of the active curve, as a track does (p. 1261). With no active curve, the row is empty.
    - The manual says that the Parameters button opens checkboxes for the curves (p. 1262).
    - The Parameters button hides or shows the names of the tracks instead. Then the tracks or the graph take the full width.
    - The ⋯ menu has Auto Zoom Graph Height. It sets the height of the graph from the keyframes, and it is on by default (p. 1264).
    - It has Off, On and Normalized. A new project has Normalized.
    - Normalized gives each curve the full height with its own range. The labels show the range of the active curve.
    - On gives all the curves one range, from the values of their keyframes.
    - Off also gives all the curves one range. A slider in the header zooms the height. A slider at the right of the graph moves the view up and down (p. 1264).
    - The Keyframe button adds a keyframe at the playhead, also outside the clip. On a keyframe, it removes that keyframe, as the Inspector's button does.
    - The Keyframe button is orange on a keyframe (p. 1250), in the Inspector too.
    - The arrows beside it go to the previous and the next keyframe of the value, also outside the clip.
    - On the Speed row, the button adds a keyframe only inside the clip, after its first frame. The arrows go only to keyframes inside the clip.
    - Reset Keyframes removes all keyframes of the track.
  - **Time** (p. 1258):
    - The time ruler shows timecode. A click or a drag on it moves the playhead.
    - The tracks start at the range of the clip. Full Extend Zoom goes back to that range.
    - Detail Zoom zooms in four times, around the playhead.
    - The zoom slider sets the zoom, around the middle of the range.
    - Ctrl and the mouse wheel zoom at the pointer. Shift and the mouse wheel move the range.
    - A scroll bar under the tracks shows the part of the clip in the range. A drag on it moves the range.
    - A click on the scroll bar outside that part moves the range to the click.
    - An arrow at the edge of a track marks its keyframes outside the range, for example after a trim.
  - **Edits:**
    - A drag on a keyframe moves it in time. A drag on a curve point also changes the value.
    - Shift and a drag on a curve point change only the time or only the value (p. 1262). The direction of the first movement selects which one.
    - A drag on a Bezier handle also moves the other handle of that keyframe. The two are then on one line.
      - The other handle goes to the opposite direction on the screen and keeps its length on the screen.
      - The value scale of the curve stays during the drag.
      - With Alt, the handle moves alone ([X161](deviations.md#x161-alt-moves-a-handle-alone-and-the-other-handle-stops-at-a-keyframe)).
    - A drag on the line between two keyframes moves the two keyframes together.
      - It changes only their values, and the two become the selection.
    - A click on the line clears the selection. A double-click on it adds a keyframe.
    - Alt and a click on the line adds a keyframe at that point.
    - Alt and a press on the line also adds a keyframe. The new keyframe does not follow the drag.
    - The ⋯ menu has Enable Snapping (p. 1264). It is on by default. With it on, a dragged control point goes to the playhead.
      - A keyframe of another curve and a timeline marker are not targets.
      - vidcut: snapping acts on a track, a group track and a curve.
      - vidcut: it does not act on a handle, or on a Shift-drag that changes only the value.
    - A drag on a group track moves that group's keyframes at that time.
    - A drag on an In or Out handle changes that handle. A handle value moves the key's own value.
    - On Position, Zoom and the anchor point, Y keeps the handle frames and uses a value offset of 0.
    - One click selects one keyframe. A click on an empty part clears the selection.
    - Ctrl-click (Cmd-click on the Mac) adds a keyframe to the selection (p. 1257). Ctrl-click on a selected keyframe removes it from the selection.
    - Shift-click selects that keyframe alone, as a plain click does.
    - A drag from an empty part of the tracks draws a box (p. 1257). On release, the keyframes in the box are the selection.
      - The box replaces the selection, also with Shift or Ctrl.
      - One box takes the keyframes of all the values in it.
    - Delete removes the selected keyframes (p. 1258).
    - After a click in the tab or the tray, Ctrl+A selects all the keyframes of the shown item (p. 1262). Ctrl+Shift+A clears that selection.
      - Elsewhere, these keys select all the clips or none.
      - A request acts on all the keyframes of a clip when it names no frames.
    - Mark > Move Selected Keyframes Left and Right move the selected keyframes by one frame (p. 1258). They send `keyframes.move`.
    - Mark > Move Selected Keyframes Up and Down change their values by one step. They send `keyframes.set` with `by`.
      - The manual says that a step is one unit (p. 4343).
      - Position, the anchor point and each crop side: 0.001 of the picture. On a 1920 px wide picture, this is 1.92 px.
        - Position X counts in the timeline frame. The anchor point X and the crop count in the fitted picture.
      - Zoom: 0.2, that is 20%. Rotation: 0.02°. Pitch and Yaw: 0.001. Crop softness: 0.2. Opacity: 0.2%. Volume: 0.2 dB.
      - The Inspector shows Pitch and Yaw with three decimals, so one step shows.
      - On Position, Zoom and the anchor point, the step goes to X and to Y.
      - A value stops at its limit. The rest of that step is lost.
    - A click on the name of a value selects its row alone. Ctrl-click and Shift-click do the same.
      - A click on a keyframe also adds its row to the selected rows. A click on an empty part does not change the rows.
      - A selected row has a lighter background over its full width.
      - The tab and the tray share the selected rows. A new clip in the tab clears them.
    - Mark > Add Keyframe (Ctrl+[) adds a keyframe at the playhead on each selected row, at the value there. It sends `keyframes.add`.
      - A row with a keyframe at the playhead does not change.
      - The Volume row takes a keyframe also outside the clip. A held key adds once.
      - The Speed row takes a keyframe only inside the clip, after its first frame.
      - The manual says that Add Keyframe works also during playback, and that the context picks the value (pp. 1254–1255). Its order is the retime controls, the open curve of the editor, the Inspector control changed last, and the audio gain.
      - The selected row picks it instead.
      - The value changed last in the Inspector and the curve in view do not pick it.
    - Mark > Delete Keyframe (Alt+]) removes the selected keyframes. With none selected, it removes the keyframes of the selected rows at the playhead.
    - Mark > Delete All Keyframes removes the selected keyframes. With none selected, it asks first. Then it removes all the keyframes of the selected rows.
      - The question names the values, the number of keyframes and the clip ([X94](deviations.md#x94-speed-keyframes-and-the-mark-menus-keyframe-items)). Cancel has the focus.
    - These three items act on the clip that the tab shows.
    - Resolve's Mark menu also has Add Static Keyframe (Ctrl+]) and a Keyframe Timeline Mode submenu. On the Cut page, both are off.
    - With the tab and the tray closed, they act on the speed keyframes of the selected clips (D48, X94).
    - With the tray open, the tab closed, and no row or keyframe selected, Add Keyframe adds a speed keyframe (X94).
    - After a click in the Keyframes tab or the keyframe tray, Ctrl+C and Ctrl+X copy or cut the selected keyframes (p. 1258).
    - Ctrl+V there pastes them on the selected clips (p. 1258).
      - The first keyframe goes at the playhead. The others keep their distance from it.
      - With more than one clip selected, each clip takes the keyframes at the same frames from its start. The earliest selected clip sets these frames.
      - A pasted keyframe can be past the clip's ends (p. 1258).
      - `keyframes.copy`, `keyframes.cut` and `keyframes.paste` do this.
    - Keyframes and clips share one clipboard. A copy of keyframes replaces the copied clips, and a copy of clips replaces the copied keyframes.
    - The ⋯ menu has Reverse Keyframes (pp. 1264, 1267). It runs the selected keyframes backward over their span.
      - Each keyframe goes as far from the last as it was from the first. It keeps its value, and it stays selected.
      - Its In and Out handles change places. Thus Ease In becomes Ease Out.
      - The span is that of all the selected keyframes. Thus the timing between the values stays.
      - A reversed keyframe takes the place of a keyframe on its new frame.
      - Speed keyframes do not reverse. `keyframes.reverse` does this.
      - Each place pastes only its own kind. Ctrl+V in the timeline pastes no keyframes, and Ctrl+V in the tab or the tray pastes no clips.
    - The header has the buttons Linear, Ease In, Ease In and Out, and Ease Out (pp. 1262–1263). Each sets its ease on all the selected keyframes.
      - A button is off when no selected keyframe takes its ease.
      - The eases are the curves of the volume line (X74). The anchor point's keyframes take no ease (D92).
    - A right-click on a keyframe of the editor opens no menu, on the Cut page and on the Edit page.
    - vidcut: a right-click on a keyframe also sets the ease. On a selected keyframe, it sets the ease of all the selected keyframes.
    - vidcut: with two or more keyframes selected, the bar under the tracks has an ease list. It shows `Mixed` when their eases are not the same.
      - In a selection, Ease In removes the Out handle of a first keyframe, and it gets no In handle. Ease Out on a last keyframe does the opposite.
    - In Resolve, a double-click on a row adds nothing, inside or outside the clip.
    - The Add Keyframes tool of Resolve's Curve Editor adds a keyframe with a click, inside the clip or before its start.
    - vidcut: a double-click on an empty part of a track or its curve adds a keyframe at the frame under the pointer, inside the clip.
      - A double-click outside the clip adds nothing.
      - The playhead moves to the new keyframe.
    - The curve of a value shows under its track (X90).
    - vidcut: the bar under the tracks shows the selected keyframe: the name of the value, the time, the value, the ease and Delete.
      - The value is in the units of the Inspector.
      - With two or more keyframes selected, the bar shows their number, their ease and Delete.
    - A keyframe that the Keyframe button or a double-click adds is selected. Thus the bar shows it at once.
  - **Keyframe tray** (p. 1259):
    - The Keyframes button of the lower timeline's toolbar shows a tray under the tracks of the lower timeline. The upper timeline does not change.
    - The tray has a track for each value of the item selected first that has keyframes. Its time is the time of the lower timeline.
    - Its tracks work as the tracks of the Keyframes tab, with Reset Keyframes and without the curve. They share the selection of the tab.
    - `gui.show keyframe_tray` opens the tray. `gui.close keyframe_tray` closes it.
    - Resolve's tray has more. vidcut does not have these parts ([todo](todo/keyframes.md)):
      - A divider that changes the height of the tray.
      - Group tracks for each effect, under a track with the clip's name.
      - A curve view, and a Parameters menu that chooses the shown values.
      - With no clip selected, the clip under the playhead. With more than one clip selected, a group for each clip.
  - The Sync Bin tab ([§7.4](#74-sync-bin-pp-708710)).
  - vidcut's bar also has Jobs (deviation X3) and Help.
  - vidcut: F1, or Help in the top bar, opens the Help window.
    - It lists every shortcut, with one action in each row.
    - A ? beside a less obvious action explains it in a few plain words.
    - Under the shortcuts, it tells in a few words what each upscaler and each Retime Process does.
    - It lists only the choices that this computer has. It does not list Auto and Project Settings.
  - Items that do not fit a narrow window go into a ⋯ menu at the right end of the bar (D72).
- **Themes:**
  - vidcut has ten built-in themes. The user can add themes as TOML files.
  - Classic, the default, is a flat dark gray, as on the Cut page.
  - Native follows the light or dark mode and the accent color of the computer.
  - Preferences or `preferences set appearance.theme=…` change the theme at once.
- **Below the Media Pool:** the edit-function buttons (§7), and Cut and Dissolve (§8.11).
- **Viewer:**
  - The mode buttons are at the top left. The Duration field is at the top right.
  - Along the bottom (p. 640): the jog control, Previous Edit, Stop, Play, Next Edit, Loop, Mark In, Mark Out and the timecode.
  - vidcut also has Clear there. It clears the marks (Alt+X).
  - An audio meter is on the right (p. 642).
  - Items that do not fit a narrow viewer go into a ⋯ menu (D72).
- **Timeline menus** (pp. 645–646):
  - **Timeline Options:**
    - Ripple On, Snap, Fixed Playhead, Trim to Audio, Trim with Safe Edit.
    - Display Clip Names, Durations and Status.
    - Minimize Subtitle Track, Boring Detector.
    - vidcut also has Filmstrips and Waveforms.
  - **Timeline Actions**, in this order:
    - Create Subtitles from Audio, Detect Scene Cuts, Audio Assistant, Voice Convert.
    - Add Video/Audio/Subtitle Track, Split Clips, Join Match Edit Clips, Add Marker, Set Color and Add Marker.
    - Clear Transition from All Edits, Add Dissolve to All Edits, Add Transition to All Edits.
    - The Add Tracks window takes 0 to 99 video tracks and 0 to 999 audio tracks. `track.add` takes the same.
    - vidcut has these items in this order, except the four in [the gaps](todo/gaps.md).
    - After them, vidcut has Add Title (D66), Add Subtitle, Re-time Subtitles to Speech (§8.12) and Ripple Delete Silence (§9).
  - **Timeline** in the menu bar: Create Subtitles from Audio is under AI Tools (p. 1234).
    - vidcut: the menu also has Keyframes and Audio Scrubbing. The other items of Timeline Actions follow.
  - **Edit Actions:** Trim Start to Playhead, Trim End to Playhead.
- **Timeline buttons** (p. 649): Ripple On, Dynamic Trim, Split, Marker. After them is the Keyframes button, which shows the keyframe tray (p. 1259).
  - vidcut also has a Snap button among them.
  - The buttons are spread evenly across their row.
- **Upper timeline:**
  - A column at its left has a label beside each track: V1, A1, ST1 and so on (p. 643).
    - The labels are plain text, with no box. Each track row is about 18 px tall, V1 too, so each label fits.
    - A click on the label of a video or audio track makes it the target track (p. 643).
    - A click on the label of a subtitle track opens the track in the Inspector.
  - The Timeline Options, Timeline Actions and Edit Actions icons are to the left of the labels. They are spread evenly across the box.
- **Video Only and Audio Only** (p. 694):
  - They are two icons at the left end of the row of edit buttons, above the upper timeline. Video Only is first.
  - The two cannot both be on.
  - `gui.only` sets them. The choice lasts for this session.

## 3. Media Pool (Ch. 34, pp. 653–675)

- **Import** (p. 654):
  - The Import Media and Import Media Folder buttons are at the top of the Media Pool.
  - Ctrl+I, the Import Media button, or right-click > Import Media opens a file dialog. The dialog can select many files.
  - **Import Media Folder**, the button beside Import Media, opens a folder dialog (pp. 654, 658; D53):
    - It brings the folder in as a bin inside the open bin, with a bin for each folder in it.
    - Then it opens that bin.
    - The Cut page has no File-menu or right-click item for it.
    - Keyboard Customization lists no command with that name.
    - vidcut adds both items.
  - vidcut: file dialogs for media, projects and exports each start in the folder that the user last used for them.
  - **Drag and drop:**
    - Clips dragged from the file system go into the Media Pool, or into a bin in the Bin list (p. 364).
    - For the Cut page, the manual says macOS only (p. 372).
    - Folders dragged into the Bin list come in as bins (p. 365).
    - vidcut takes files and folders dropped anywhere on its window into the open bin (deviation X66).
    - It brings in the files as Import Media does, and each folder as Import Media Folder does.
  - In a project made without a format, the first video sets the format without a question (D37).
- **Stills and image sequences** (pp. 114, 366, 1194):
  - An image file, or a GIF or WebP of one frame, comes in as a still (D38).
  - Numbered image files come in as one clip (D39).
- **Remove** (p. 654):
  - Delete or Backspace removes the selected items from the pool, after a confirmation.
  - Remove from Pool on the right-click menu asks no question.
  - Only the pool items go. The files on disk stay.
  - The clips and the compound clips that use an item stay, on the timeline and in the pool.
  - Those clips are offline. They are red on the timeline, and they play no sound.
  - Their picture is Media Offline, in a dark red 16:9 box with a warning sign. The box is in the middle of a black frame.
    - The box fits the frame of the timeline. The transform and the crop of the clip do not apply.
    - The opacity and the composite mode of the clip apply. In a compound clip, the picture is the same.
  - Before an export of a timeline with offline clips, vidcut asks for a confirmation (p. 4130). The export then shows them as Media Offline.
  - Import of the same file puts the item back in the pool. Its clips are then online again.
  - vidcut also puts back the removed subclips of that file (not measured).
- **Views** (pp. 638, 661–665):
  - **Thumbnail:** a hover scrubs the clip, and I/O mark its source In/Out. A click on the lower-right corner of a thumbnail shows its metadata (deviation X60).
  - **Filmstrip:** each clip is a row of its pictures, as wide as the pool, under its name. Hover and I/O work as on a thumbnail.
  - **List:** a column for each field. A click on a header sorts by that field.
  - **Metadata** (pp. 661–663): a card for each clip, in as many columns as fit the pool.
    - A thumbnail is at the left of the card. A hover on it scrubs the clip, and I/O mark its source In/Out.
    - Beside the thumbnail, from the top:
      - The main description, which follows the sort (p. 662):
        - Timecode and Clip Color give Date Created and the start timecode.
        - Camera gives the camera name and the start timecode.
        - Date Time, Date Modified and Date Imported give Date Created and the file name.
        - Scene, Shot gives the scene, the shot and the take.
        - Each other sort gives the clip name.
      - The start timecode, Date Created and the camera name, such as Cam A. A file with no timecode shows 00:00:00:00.
      - The scene, the shot and the take.
      - The clip name and the comment.
      - An empty value shows `---`.
      - A subtitle file shows its cue count and its length on the second line. It shows only its name on the last line.
    - Sorted by Clip Name, the clips are in clusters by the first letter of the name, each under a heading (pp. 662–663).
      - Inside a cluster, the order is by timecode. The Source Tape takes this order.
    - Sorted by Date Modified, the clusters are the days of the file's modification time (p. 662).
    - Sorted by Timecode, the clusters are the days of Date Created (p. 662). The order is by day and then by timecode.
    - Sorted by Camera, the clusters are the camera names, under headings such as Camera A (pp. 662–663). The order is by name and then by timecode.
    - Sorted by Date Time, the clusters are the days of Date Created (p. 662). The order is by day and then by timecode.
    - Sorted by Bin, the clusters are the bins (p. 662). A heading shows the full path of the bin. The order is by bin and then by timecode.
    - Sorted by Scene, Shot, the clusters are the scenes, under headings such as Scene 1 (pp. 662–663). The order is by scene, shot and take.
    - Sorted by Clip Color, the clusters are the color names (p. 662). The order is by color and then by timecode.
    - Sorted by Online Status, the clusters are Original and Proxy (p. 662). A clip is in Proxy when it has a proxy that fits its file.
    - A clip with no value for the sort has no heading. In ascending order, these clips come first.
    - Sorted by Date Imported or Duration, there are no clusters. vidcut keeps no date of import ([todo](todo/gaps.md)).
    - Descending reverses the whole order (p. 663).
    - `pool.set` with `view` `metadata` opens this view.
  - A sound file shows its waveform.
  - **Metadata Display** (p. 665): a tag icon beside the search field shows metadata of the selected clip at the top of the pool. The metadata is the camera name, scene, shot and take, frame rate and resolution.
    - vidcut: the strip also shows the codec, the bits and chroma subsampling, SDR or HDR, and the length.
    - It also shows the start timecode, the sound and the file size.
- **Sort and search** (pp. 666–667):
  - Sort Media By has Clip Name, Date Imported, Date Modified, Timecode (D59), Camera, Date Time, Bin, Scene Shot, Clip Color and Online Status. Each is ascending or descending.
  - vidcut also sorts by Duration.
  - This order is also the Source Tape order.
  - The search field filters live, through the open bin and the bins inside it.
- **Timelines** (pp. 678, 838–841):
  - A project holds many timelines. The pool shows each one in its bin, with its length. The open one is highlighted.
  - A double-click on a timeline opens it. The edits then act on it.
  - In/Out marks, markers, subtitle tracks and the playhead stay with each timeline.
  - All timelines share one undo history. Opening a timeline is a step of it: "Set Timeline 2 as Current Timeline".
  - The Timeline Selection menu, at the top of the Timeline viewer, shows the name of the open timeline. It lists the 10 timelines opened last, the last first.
  - **New Timeline** (File > New Timeline, Ctrl+N, or Create New Timeline in the menu of the pool):
    - Start Timecode, 01:00:00:00 by default.
    - Timeline Name, "Timeline N" by default. N is the count of timelines plus 1, or the next number that no timeline has.
    - Video Tracks and Audio Tracks, 1 each.
    - Use Project Settings, on by default. Off, the timeline has its own frame rate, resolution, drop-frame and color space. It has no LUT of its own.
    - The timeline goes into the open bin, and opens.
  - The menu of a timeline in the pool has Open, Duplicate Timeline, Rename, Timeline Settings and Delete Timeline.
    - Duplicate Timeline makes "X copy", then "X copy 1", in the same bin, and opens it. The copy has the markers, and no In/Out marks.
    - Timeline Settings has the start timecode, Use Project Settings and the own format.
    - Delete Timeline asks first. When the open timeline goes, the timeline made before it opens, else the one after it. A project can have no timeline.
  - An edit into a project with no timeline makes a timeline (p. 678). vidcut gives it the New Timeline defaults: the next free name, and 1 video track and 1 audio track.
    - Each command of a batch (`apply`) follows this rule as it does alone.
  - A timeline in the project locks the frame rate and drop-frame of the project. Media in the pool do not lock them.
    - Thus the first video of a project with a timeline sets only the picture size (D37).
    - The manual says that media lock the frame rate (pp. 141, 364). They do not.
    - With no timeline, they change again.
  - A timeline with its own settings changes its frame rate and drop-frame only while it has no clips. Its start timecode keeps its label.
    - Its markers, subtitles and In/Out marks keep their time.
  - A compound clip made in a timeline at another rate is converted by time where it goes. Pasted clips are too.
  - File > Import > Timeline makes a new timeline, named after the file, in the open bin, and opens it. A name that a timeline has is refused.
    - The Import window has the timeline's name. vidcut also offers Append, Replace, As a Compound Clip and Media Only.
    - Append or Replace into a project with no timeline first makes "Timeline 1", as an edit does. It takes the file's start timecode.
    - A file at another frame rate than its timeline keeps its frame numbers.
      - The timeline has the project's settings. Each clip has the same first frame and the same length in frames as in the file.
      - Each clip starts at the source time that the file gives. Each timeline frame then moves the source by one frame of the file's rate.
      - Thus a 25 fps file plays at 120% speed in a 30 fps timeline.
      - The pictures are the ones that the numbers of the file give.
      - The start is the same count of frames. Thus 01:00:00:00 at 25 fps is 00:50:00:00 at 30 fps.
      - The import gives a warning with the two rates and the speed.
- **Clip Info** (vidcut):
  - Clip Info shows all that vidcut knows of a file.
  - The info button of the metadata strip or of a thumbnail corner opens it. The menu of a pool item also opens it.
  - It reads the file again for its container and streams: codecs and profiles, bit rates, color tags, channel layouts, languages and tags (`media.details`).
  - Copy puts all of it on the clipboard as text.
- **Bins** (pp. 655–656, 377):
  - The Master bin is the top level.
  - The pool shows the clips of the open bin, and its bins as folders.
  - A path at the top (Master › Interviews › Day 1), the Bins menu and a double-click open any bin.
  - New Bin is on the File menu (Shift+Ctrl+N), on the Bins menu and on the right-click menu of the pool.
  - The menu of a bin has Rename Bin and Delete Bin.
  - Delete Bin deletes the bin and the bins in it (p. 377). It also removes the media in them, as Remove does. The compound clips and the synced groups in them go too.
  - Delete Bin asks no question, and the clips that use the media of the bin stay, offline.
  - Clips and bins dragged onto a bin, or onto a level of the path, move into it.
  - The menu of a clip has Move to Bin and Create Bin With Selected Clips (p. 656).
  - Imports go into the open bin (p. 377).
  - Source Tape strings together the clips of the open bin and of its bins, in the sort order (pp. 680, 696).
- **Clip Color** (pp. 667–668):
  - Right-click clips in the pool or the timeline. Then select one of 16 colors, or Clear Color.
  - A timeline clip has its own color. If it has none, it has the color of its pool item.
- **Camera name** (p. 706):
  - A camera can record its name in the file, and Resolve reads it. The Camera # field of the Metadata Editor sets it by hand.
  - A media item has a camera name (`camera`). It is text, such as a letter or a number.
  - The Camera row of Clip Info edits it. `media.set` sets it on one or more items. An empty name clears it.
  - `media.get` and the metadata strip show it.
  - vidcut does not read the name from a file.
- **Date Created, Scene, Shot, Take and Comment** (pp. 661–663, 750):
  - A media item can have `created`, `scene`, `shot`, `take` and `comment`. Each is text, and `created` has the form `YYYY-MM-DD`.
  - The rows of Clip Info edit them. `media.set` sets them on one or more items. An empty value clears the field.
  - `media.get` shows them. The metadata strip shows the scene, the shot and the take.
  - vidcut does not read Date Created from a file.
- **Input LUT** (p. 3480, and [§13](#the-lut-tables)):
  - A media item with pictures has an Input LUT (`input_lut`). Each of its clips on the timeline gets it in place of the Input LUT of the project.
  - The `LUT` submenu of the pool's right-click menu and the LUT section of the Inspector set it. They list the LUTs of the LUT folders ([§13](#the-lut-tables)).
  - `media.set` sets it on one or more items. An empty path clears it. `media.get` shows it.
  - A generator, a title and a compound clip have no media item. Thus they have no Input LUT.
- **Multicam layout** (p. 1037):
  - `multicam.layout` tells where the files of a multicam clip lie. It changes nothing, and the project does not store the result.
  - The answer has rows. A named row holds all files of one camera name. Named rows come first, in camera order.
  - Rows with no name follow. A file with no name never joins a named row.
    - By `timecode`, files with no name go in timecode order. With one timecode, they go in the order of the request.
    - By `timecode`, each of these files goes on the first row with no name where it does not overlap. Touching is no overlap.
    - Example: tags 01:00:00:00, 01:00:05:00, 01:00:10:00 and 01:01:00:00 give three rows. Row 1 holds the first and the last file.
    - By `in`, `out` or `sound`, each file with no name has its own row, although two of them may not overlap.
    - A file with no name and no picture has its own row, after the other rows. It has sound only.
  - `sync` is `timecode`, `in`, `out` or `sound`.
  - By `timecode`, each file starts at its start timecode. A file with no timecode starts at 00:00:00:00.
    - The length is the union of the files, with any gaps. A gap of hours is not an error.
    - The start timecode of the whole is the earliest file.
  - By `in` or `out`, the marks of the items line up. The start timecode of the whole is 01:00:00:00.
    - One mark counts for each row. By `in`, it is the In of the file with the earliest timecode. By `out`, it is the Out of the file with the latest timecode.
    - The other files of the row keep their timecode distance from that file. The order of the request plays no part.
  - **The row rule:** files of one named row do not overlap.
    - Files go in timecode order. With one timecode, they go in the order of the request.
    - By `sound`, files go by their places from the sound. A tie goes by the order of the request ([deviation X125](deviations.md#x125-sync-by-sound-uses-time-order-on-a-row)).
    - A file keeps only the part after everything before it. A file with no such part is not on the row, and `dropped` names it.
    - A file does not move. It loses frames at its start, and `head` gives those frames.
    - Example: tags 01:00:05:00 and 01:00:10:00 on one row, 750 frames each. The first stays whole. The second keeps 125 frames from 01:00:35:00.
  - An item with marks uses only the frames between them. The shift stays. `head` and `tail` give the frames that it does not use.
  - `full_extents` uses each whole file. By `in` or `out`, its marks only line it up.
  - A request that names a file twice counts it once.
  - A still takes part. It starts at 00:00:00:00 and has the length of a new still.
  - An item with one mark uses the frames from In to its end, or from its start to Out.
  - By `in`, an item with no In lines up at its first frame. By `out`, an item with no Out lines up at its end.
- **Sync by sound** (pp. 706–707):
  - `sync` `sound` lines up files by their sound. `multicam.layout` and `multicam.create` take it.
  - A shift is in whole frames. Half a frame rounds up.
  - One shift holds for the whole file. vidcut does not follow drift. The shift falls between the error at the start and the error at the end.
  - The start timecode of the whole is 01:00:00:00.
  - A file that matches no other file stays at the start of the whole. `unmatched` names it. A file with no sound is such a file.
    - Thus with two files and no match, both are at the start and `unmatched` names both.
  - Two sets of files that do not match each other do not overlap. The later set starts one frame after the earlier one ends.
  - The whole files line up. An item with marks then uses only the frames between them, at the place of those frames.
    - The empty front stays. Thus marks at 100 to 400 on both files, 250 frames apart, give 650 frames.
  - vidcut compares the first sound stream of each file, with all its channels (not measured).
  - Each file is placed by its own sound. The row rule then acts in the order of the places ([deviation X125](deviations.md#x125-sync-by-sound-uses-time-order-on-a-row)).
    - Thus of two files that overlap on a row, the earlier file stays whole. The later file loses its head.
    - A file that starts after the other file ends keeps all its frames.
    - The order of the request counts only in a tie. Of two files with one place and one length, the first in the order of the request stays whole. The second file is not on the row.
  - `multicam.layout` also gives `pairs`: for each two files the shift, the score and whether they match.
  - While the GUI runs, `multicam.create` by sound is a job.
- **Synced group** (p. 708):
  - `multicam.create` stores files as a synced group, a pool item with the name "Media Sync", the date and the time. `name` gives another name.
  - It keeps the rows of `multicam.layout` with `full_extents`. A file's marks and its start timecode do not change.
  - The group stores its rows. A camera name that changes later does not move a file until Auto Sync reads the names again.
  - A stored file has its visible part: the frames lost at its head and at its tail. A file with no visible part is not in the group.
  - The group keeps `dropped`: the files that the row rule or a drag took off its rows.
  - The files stay in their bins. `bin` tells where the group goes, and the top level is the default.
  - A file can be in more than one group. The newest group that holds the file counts.
  - `multicam.list` gives each group with its layout. `media.get` and `media.list` give `group` for a file in a group.
  - `multicam.rename` and `multicam.move` act on the group. `multicam.remove` removes the group, and its files stay.
  - A group can have one file.
  - `media.remove` also takes the file out of its groups. A group with no file left goes too ([deviation X128](deviations.md#x128-removing-a-file-takes-it-out-of-its-groups)).
  - No command places a group on the timeline.
  - The pool does not show a group as an item. A file in a group has a blue sync badge.
  - `multicam.set` sets the rows and the sync locks of a group ([§7.5](#75-sync-clips-window-pp-706708)). `multicam.create` takes them as `rows`, and the group then starts at 01:00:00:00.
    - They refuse files of one row that overlap, and a file with no visible part. A file that no row names is not in the group.
  - `multicam.set` and `multicam.create` with `rows` apply the rules of far gaps and of the earliest row. `at` plus `hidden` is the place of the first visible frame in real time.
  - `multicam.create` without `rows` keeps each gap. Its start is the first timecode, or 01:00:00:00 by sound, In or Out.
  - `multicam.place` moves one file as a drag does, by the rules of overlap in [§7.5](#75-sync-clips-window-pp-706708). It refuses a file on a row with the sync lock.
- **Input Color Space** (pp. 228, 230):
  - The color tags of a file tell how to read the colors of its pictures.
  - For a file with wrong tags, right-click the media in the pool. Select BT.709, BT.601, BT.2100 PQ or BT.2100 HLG to read it that way.
  - As Tagged uses the tags again.
  - The viewer, the thumbnails and the exports follow this setting (D35).
- **Alpha Mode** (pp. 451, 1205):
  - Alpha Mode tells how to read the alpha channel of a clip: None, Premultiplied, Straight or Invert. Invert is the fourth choice of the list.
  - Alpha channels are on by default (p. 473). A clip with no alpha channel does not show these choices (p. 451).
  - Right-click the media in the pool, or a clip on the timeline (p. 1205).
  - It is a setting of the media item. Thus it applies to every clip of that item.
  - `media.set` sets `alpha_mode`. It refuses a media item with no alpha channel.
  - A clip of color c and alpha α is over a picture b. The result is:
    - None: c. The clip is opaque.
    - Straight: b + (c − b) × α.
    - Premultiplied: c + b × (1 − α), at most 1.
    - Invert: b + (c − b) × (1 − α).
  - A ProRes 4444 with alpha and no tags is Straight.
  - vidcut reads the alpha channel of each file as Straight until `media.set` changes it.
- **Upscale** (pp. 288, 432–433; deviation [X91](deviations.md#x91-upscale-uses-the-upscaler-of-the-gpu)):
  - Upscale enlarges the pictures of a media item with a better upscaler before they are fitted.
  - It is a setting of the media item. Thus it applies to every clip of that item (p. 433).
  - Right-click the media in the pool, or use the Upscale section of the Inspector.
  - Upscale is Off or On.
  - Its Upscaler is Project Settings, the default, AI or Classic. Project Settings has the upscaler of the project: Auto, AI or Classic.
    - AI is a neural network on the GPU. It has no settings.
    - Classic has a Sharpness of Low, Medium or High, which selects the upscaler.
  - The first time that a change needs an AI upscaler, vidcut asks to download its weights.
  - AI takes the picture in the timeline color space (§13). In an SDR timeline, HDR media is tone-mapped first.
  - In an HDR timeline, AI Slow refuses HDR media, and the Inspector shows a warning (X98).
  - A placed picture is made at most 4 times as large as its source, before it is placed.
  - A picture that fills the frame takes the better upscaler for all of its enlargement.
  - Upscale works only where a picture is enlarged. A picture that is made smaller is scaled as before.
  - View > Show Upscale shows it in the viewer (`viewer.upscale`). It is off by default, as it costs playback speed.
  - With AI, the viewer then shows the result of the AI upscaler, as the export makes it.
  - `media.set` sets `upscale`, `upscaler` and `sharpness`. With `download`, it downloads the weights first.
- **Relinking media** (pp. 668–669):
  - An item whose file is missing has the mark MISSING.
  - Its clips are red on the timeline (pp. 509, 530), and the viewer shows Media Offline.
  - Relink Media finds the files in a folder that you select, and in its subfolders.
  - Relink… on a clip selects one file.
  - vidcut checks each file that it finds (deviation X1).
- **Interaction:**
  - A click selects a clip.
  - In Source Clip mode, the Viewer shows the clip. In Source Tape mode, the playhead jumps to it (pp. 680, 704).
  - A double-click opens it as Source Clip (p. 680).
- **Selection** (p. 719):
  - Click, Shift-click range, Ctrl-click toggle, Ctrl+A.
  - A drag from an empty part of the pool draws a box. The box replaces the selection with the clips that it touches.
  - With Ctrl held, the box adds each clip that is not selected, and removes each clip that is selected.
  - vidcut:
    - A drag on a clip stays a drag of that clip (§7.3). A drag on a bin stays a drag of that bin (not measured).
    - The selection changes on release. A box that touches nothing clears the selection (not measured).
    - The box takes clips, not bins (not measured).
    - Shift has no effect on the box (not measured).
    - The box does not move the Viewer, as Ctrl-click and Shift-click do not (not measured).
    - The wheel scrolls the pool during a drag, and the box keeps its start. The pool does not scroll by itself at its edge (not measured).
  - `selection.set` with `media` sets the same selection by request.

## 4. Viewer (pp. 639–640, 679–693, 713)

### 4.1 Modes
- **Source Clip** (p. 680):
  - The Viewer shows the selected pool clip.
  - It steps through the frames of the clip, at the frame rate of the clip.
    - The source marks of the clip are on these frames.
    - vidcut puts the markers of the clip on these frames too.
    - A still or a sound file has the frame rate of the project.
  - A scroll area under the picture stands for the whole clip. It has the playhead, a zoomed-in waveform and In/Out handles that you can drag.
  - vidcut draws the waveform of the whole clip across the scroll area, from the bottom edge up.
    - With marks, the waveform is green between In and Out, and gray outside them.
    - Without marks, the waveform is green.
    - The waveform does not zoom ([gaps](todo/gaps.md)).
  - Jog In and Jog Out controls at its ends move the marks frame by frame (p. 692).
    - Jog In is at the left of the scroll area. Jog Out is at the right.
    - A drag moves that mark. 8 pixels is one frame (D97).
    - A click moves that mark one frame later.
    - `media.marks.set` sets a clip mark. `tape.marks.set` sets a tape mark.
- **Source Tape** (pp. 680–681, 704–706):
  - All pool clips play end to end as one tape, in the pool sort order.
  - The tape steps through frames of the project.
    - A clip at another frame rate shows the frame that each frame of the tape falls in, rounded down.
    - The source timecode is that frame of the clip, at the frame rate of the clip.
  - The clip under the playhead becomes selected in the pool.
- **Multi Source** (pp. 679, 682):
  - The Viewer shows the video files and stills of the current bin and its subfolders in a grid. All of them follow one playhead.
  - `viewer.set` with `multi_source` opens it. It needs no synced group and no timeline.
  - An audio file gets no cell and is not counted. The title is the count and the bin, for example "3 Clips - S1".
  - Files line up by file timecode. A saved synced group plays no part.
  - The scroll area stands for all the material, and it closes the gaps between files.
  - A grid cell:
    - Each camera name has one cell. Each file with no camera name has its own cell.
    - Named cameras come first, by name. The others follow in pool order.
    - The label is "Camera", then the name. A camera with no name counts from 1 among the unnamed cameras.
    - The number at the top left stays with the camera.
    - A camera with no frame at the playhead has no cell, and the grid closes up.
    - Two files of one camera at one time show the first file.
  - The grid has the round-up of the square root of its cells as columns. It has no limit on cells.
  - One camera is active. It plays its sound, edits use it, and it has a red outline and a red corner.
    - The first camera is the default.
    - When the active camera has no frame at the playhead, the first cell becomes active. It does not come back by itself.
  - A click on a cell, or a number key, opens that camera alone (`viewer.set` with `angle` and `alone`).
    - The title is the file name, the pool selects the file, and the position stays.
    - A number key also changes the camera in that view.
    - The close button returns to the grid, with that camera active.
  - In and Out in the grid are marks of the viewer (`viewer.marks.set`, `viewer.marks.clear`). They are not on the files (p. 682).
    - A change of bin clears them, sets the position to 0 and makes the first camera active.
    - Opening a camera alone gives its file these marks, cut at the ends of the file. With no marks, the file loses its marks.
    - In and Out in the view of one camera are on that file only.
    - The Duration field takes a typed length (`viewer.marks.set` with `duration`). The Out moves to the In plus it. With no In, the In goes to the first frame. The marks hold exactly the typed length.
  - An edit in the grid takes the marks' part of the active camera. The marks of the files play no part, and the viewer's marks stay:
    - An In with no Out takes the file to its end.
    - An Out with no In counts position 0 as the In.
    - Marks outside the file: nothing happens.
    - With no marks, Append does nothing. Smart Insert adds nothing, and the playhead moves to the nearest edit. Ripple Overwrite, Place On Top and Source Overwrite take the whole file, with no empty part.
    - An In before the file: the part before the file stays empty on the timeline, before the clip (`lead`).
      - Append: after the last clip.
      - Smart Insert: at the nearest edit. Later clips move by the empty part plus the clip.
      - Ripple Overwrite: from the start of the clip at the playhead, or from the timeline In. Later clips move.
      - Place On Top: from the playhead, or from the timeline In.
    - Place On Top and Ripple Overwrite keep the clip's length between timeline marks.
    - Source Overwrite has no empty part, because it lines up by file timecode (`grid`):
      - The reference is the clip under the playhead if it shows part of the marks, or of the file with no marks. Else it is the first clip that does.
      - The playhead goes to the clip's end, also on a new track.
  - An edit in the view of one camera uses the marks of the file. With no marks, it takes the whole file. Then the grid comes back.
  - Escape leaves the view of one camera, as the close button does.
  - Multi Source keeps its own active camera and position. A clip open in Source Clip does not change them.
  - F10 Overwrite in the grid places the marks' part at the playhead, with no empty part. With no marks, it places the whole file (not measured).
  - A bin with no video files opens an empty grid (not measured).
- **Timeline** (p. 681):
  - The Viewer shows the timeline at its playhead, with no scroll area.
  - Icons mark the first and the last frame of a clip.
- **Switching:**
  - Q toggles between Timeline and the last source mode (p. 696).
  - A click or a drag in the timeline area switches to Timeline (p. 681).

### 4.2 Transport (pp. 640, 687–688, 870–872)
- The buttons are those of the viewers of the Edit page (p. 870): Previous, Play Reverse, Stop, Play Forward, Next and Loop.
  - In vidcut, Play Reverse or Play Forward is lit while the viewer plays in its direction.
- §11 gives the keys.
- J/K/L shuttle (pp. 696, 871; D54):
  - Each repeated J or L makes the speed higher, from 2× to 64×.
  - K held with J or L plays slowly.
  - K held with a tap of J or L steps a frame.
- Ctrl+/ loops playback (D51).
- A drag on the jog control scrubs finely (p. 640, D97).
  - The Jog control is on the viewer transport, before Previous.
  - A drag of 8 pixels moves one frame.
  - `player.seek` moves the playhead the same way.

### 4.3 Timecode (pp. 640, 688, 713–714)
- **Go to a time:**
  - Click the timecode field and type.
  - Or type `=` and a timecode, or `+` or `-` and a value to jump by.
  - Digits fill a timecode from the right. Each field takes two digits: the hours, the minutes, the seconds and the frames.
  - A field that the digits do not reach keeps its value from the timecode that the field shows.
  - Example: `305` on `01:00:12:10` gives `01:00:03:05`.
  - A value to jump by starts from zero. Thus `+100` jumps 1 second, and `+10000` jumps 1 minute.
  - At 119.88 and 120 fps, the frames take three digits. Thus `+1000` is 1 second, and `+100` is 100 frames.
  - The same rule applies in the Duration of Speed Change, the In and Out of a subtitle, and Edit Subclip.
  - The field of Change Clip Duration starts empty. Thus its digits count from zero.
  - A value with a unit (`5s`, `450f`) is that duration.
- **Display:**
  - The frames of a timecode have as many digits as the last frame of a second: two, or three at 119.88 and 120 fps.
  - Thus `01:00:00:000` is the hour at 120 fps, and `01:00:00:00` is the hour at 100 fps.
  - The Cut and Edit pages show three digits there. The timecode field also takes three digits for the frames.
  - The source modes show source timecode. Timeline mode shows record timecode.
  - The timecode is drop-frame where the project counts drop-frame (D47).
  - That default is Auto (D91).
  - A right-click chooses another count (D91).
  - There is no KeyKode ([gaps](todo/gaps.md)).
- **Stats for Nerds** (vidcut):
  - The right-click menu of the viewer opens a panel over the picture.
  - The panel has a group for each of these:
    - decoding, the picture and how it was made
    - Upscale and Retime Process
    - the viewer, playback, the kept pictures and the sound
  - The group How It Was Made tells about the picture on the screen, also when playback is paused.
  - The Upscale group shows when the clip under the playhead has Upscale on. It names the upscaler and the factor.
  - `player stats` shows or hides the panel.

### 4.4 In/Out (pp. 691–693, 700)
- **Source In/Out:** I/O in a source mode or over a pool thumbnail, or the handles of the scroll area.
- **Timeline In/Out:**
  - I/O in Timeline mode, or while you drag the ruler of either timeline.
  - The range shows on both rulers, and you can drag it there.
- **Clearing:** Alt+X clears In and Out.
- **Duration field:**
  - It shows the marked duration.
  - A change to it moves the Out mark (p. 693; D60).
  - It takes a number of frames, + or − and a number to change it by, or a timecode.

### 4.5 Other viewer features
- Full Screen (Shift+F) fills the display with the viewer.
  - Shift+F again returns to the page.
  - The Full Screen button in the top bar does the same thing.
  - `viewer.frame` with `full_screen` or `page` does it from a script.
- Enhanced viewer (Alt+F) fills the page with the viewer.
  - The Media Pool, the timelines and the bars are covered.
  - Alt+F again returns to the page.
  - `viewer.frame` with `enhanced` does it from a script.
- Esc returns to the page from full screen and from the enhanced viewer.
- Safe areas (pp. 682–683, 1177):
  - A Safe Area control at the right of the viewer's transport toggles framing guides.
  - Its menu, and View > Safe Area, pick the framing aspect and the safe-area lines.
  - Framing aspects:
    - Social Media: 1:1, 4:5, 9:16, 1.91:1 and 16:9.
    - Broadcast and Film: Default (the timeline's aspect), 1.33, 1.66, 1.77, 1.85 and 2.35.
  - Safe-area lines, each on its own: Extents, Action, Title and Center.
  - The framing box is the chosen aspect, fitted inside the picture.
  - Default uses the picture itself.
  - Extents is the boundary of the framing box.
  - The safe-area lines use that box, also while framing guides are off.
  - Framing guides shade outside that box when it does not fill the picture.
  - Action is 93% of the extents width and height, centered. Title is 90% (D90).
  - Center is a cross through the middle of the extents.
  - The guides draw in Timeline mode and in both source modes.
  - They do not draw in the Trim Editor or during Dynamic Trim (X80).
  - An export never shows them.
  - The choice is a user preference (`viewer.guides`), not part of the project.
  - With no fields, `viewer.guides` toggles framing guides.
  - A field that is left out stays as it is. `preferences.set` does not toggle.
- Fast Review plays a long clip faster than a short clip (p. 640, D97).
  - The Fast Review button is on the viewer transport.
  - `player.fast_review` turns it on or off. It lasts for this session.
  - A clip of 10 seconds or less plays at 1×.
  - A longer clip plays at its length in seconds divided by 10, at most 8×.
  - Play and Space use that speed. J and L keep their shuttle speeds.
- Skimming with C held (p. 640).
  - Hold C and move the pointer across the picture.
  - The horizontal place of the pointer is the frame.
  - The playhead goes there with `player.seek`.
- **D97. Fast Review speed** (p. 640).
  - The manual says that a long clip plays faster than a short clip.
  - It does not give the speed.
  - A clip of 10 seconds or less plays at 1×.
  - A longer clip plays at its seconds divided by 10.
  - The speed is at most 8×.
  - 10 seconds is one short shot. 8× stays slow enough to see the picture.
  - The jog control and Jog In and Jog Out move one frame for each 8 pixels.
  - 8 pixels is a small drag, so the scrub stays fine.
- Audio scrubbing (Shift+S; on by default, p. 876):
  - A drag of the playhead, and frame steps, play the sound under it (D42).
  - Timeline > Audio Scrubbing also switches it.
- Scopes: Resolve shows them on the Color, Media and Deliver pages (pp. 3092–3093).
  - vidcut: the View menu and the header of the viewer turn on each scope by itself.
  - vidcut: they are in a column at the right of the viewer picture. The Trim Editor, Dynamic Trim, Multi Source and a synced grid show none.
  - The lines of the scales and the graticules are orange (90, 72, 20). The labels of the scales are orange (182, 144, 44).
  - vidcut: the letters of the Vectorscope have the color of the GUI text (not measured).
  - The Waveform has the modes Y, CbCr and RGB (p. 3103).
    - Y shows luma. CbCr shows the two color differences over one another. RGB shows red, green and blue over one another (p. 3097).
    - In RGB mode, the R, G and B buttons turn each graph on and off (p. 3103).
  - The Parade has the modes RGB, YRGB and YCbCr (p. 3102). It shows the graphs of a mode side by side.
  - The Option button of each scope opens the options of that scope (p. 3102). A click outside the options closes them.
    - vidcut: the button is at the corner of the scope, at the right of its name.
    - vidcut: the scopes menu also has the options of each scope, in a submenu for each scope.
  - vidcut: Waveform Options and Parade Options set the modes. `viewer.scopes` sets them too.
  - RGB is the default of the Waveform and of the Parade. R, G and B are on.
  - vidcut: a graph of red, green or blue is in its color. A graph of Y, Cb or Cr is white.
    - Resolve's Colorize changes the colors of the graphs (p. 3103). vidcut has no Colorize ([gaps](todo/gaps.md)).
  - vidcut: a color difference of zero is at the middle of the scale, code 512.
  - The Vectorscope has Show Skin Tone Indicator (p. 3103). It shows a line at the traditional In-phase angle, a guide for average skin tone.
    - The line goes from the center to the circle, at 123.14 degrees counterclockwise from the Cb axis.
    - Its color is dark gray (63, 63, 63) with the Graticule slider at the middle. It is off by default.
    - vidcut: Vectorscope Options turns it on. `viewer.scopes` with `skin_tone` turns it on too.
  - The Histogram shows how many pixels have each value, from black at the left to white at the right (p. 3099).
    - It has the modes RGB and YRGB (p. 3104). Waveform Scale Style also sets its scale (p. 3093).
    - vidcut: it has one band for each channel, from top to bottom. Each band fills the area under its curve in its color.
    - vidcut: the labels of the scale are at the top. Video Level Scopes moves its values as in the Waveform.
    - The Histogram spans the codes 0 to 1023. With Video Level Scopes, Percentage shows 0 to 100 on it, with no −10 and 110.
    - The height of a bin is linear in its share of the pixels. At the default Gain, a bin with 1/64 of the pixels fills its band.
    - A taller bin is cut at the top of its band. The Gain slider sets the height (p. 3104).
    - vidcut: a bin is one column of the scope (not measured).
    - RGB is the default. vidcut: Histogram Options sets the mode.
  - The CIE Chromaticity scope plots the chromaticity of the picture in a CIE 1931 xy graph or a CIE 1976 uv graph (pp. 3099–3100, 3104).
    - It shows the horseshoe of the visible colors, the black body locus, a triangle of the gamut and its white point (pp. 3099–3100).
    - A label names the gamut, with the coordinates of its red, green, blue and white (p. 3099).
    - The Additional Gamut adds a second triangle (p. 3104). vidcut does not have it ([gaps](todo/gaps.md)).
    - The gamut is Rec.709 with the D65 white, also on a Rec.2100 ST2084 timeline.
    - The name of the gamut is at the left of its green corner.
    - vidcut: each point of the graph is in the color of its chromaticity.
    - The axes go from 0 to 1. There is a line each 0.1 and a label each 0.2. There is no line at 0.
    - Short ticks on the two axes mark each 0.025.
    - The grid, its ticks and the labels of the axes are in the orange of the graticule.
    - The graph is a square at the left of the scope. The names of the axes are beside the 1.0 labels (pp. 3100–3101).
    - The name of the gamut and its coordinates are in a column at the right of the scope (pp. 3100–3101).
    - vidcut: the horseshoe uses the CIE 1931 2-degree observer from 380 nm to 700 nm.
    - The horseshoe has a short tick outside it each 10 nm, with no numbers.
    - The black body locus goes from about 1390 K to its end at infinite temperature.
    - The horseshoe, its ticks and the black body locus are gray (190, 190, 190). The gamut is dark gray (76, 76, 76).
    - The white point is a small circle in gray (229, 229, 229).
    - The name of the gamut and the column of coordinates are gray (152, 152, 152).
    - In a graph with a side of 1270 px, the ticks of the horseshoe are about 12 px long. The ticks of the axes are 14 px long.
    - At that size, the circle has a radius of 6 px.
    - vidcut: these lengths are in proportion to the side of the graph. The radius of the circle is 2 px or more (not measured).
    - CIE 1931 xy is the default. vidcut: CIE Chromaticity Options sets the graph.
  - Waveform Scale Style sets the scale of the Waveform, the Parade and the Histogram (p. 3093). `viewer.scopes` with `scale` sets it too.
  - The choices are 10-bit, 12-bit, Percentage, mV and HDR (ST.2084/HLG). 10-bit is the default.
  - Each scale spans the full range of the code values.
  - 10-bit has a line each 128, 12-bit each 512, Percentage each 10, and mV each 100.
  - HDR has lines at 0, 1, 10, 100, 1000 and 10000 nits on the PQ curve.
  - HDR also has dashed lines with no label between those lines.
    - The dashed lines are at 26, 203 and 2000 nits (p. 3092).
  - HDR is the same scale on a Rec.2100 ST2084, a Rec.2100 HLG and a Rec.709 timeline. It uses the PQ curve.
    - The manual says that without color management, the HDR scale always shows HDR levels (p. 256).
  - The same menu has Video Level Scopes and Data Level Scopes (p. 3095). Data Level Scopes is the default.
    - Video Level Scopes changes only what the scopes show. The monitor and the export do not change (p. 3095).
    - The figure on p. 3095 shows full-range white at code 940 of the 10-bit scale with Video Level Scopes.
    - With Video Level Scopes, the Waveform and the Parade show black at code 64 and white at code 940.
    - The lines and labels of 10-bit stay where they are.
    - vidcut: the lines of 12-bit stay too (not measured).
    - The lines and labels of Percentage, mV and HDR move with the picture.
      - Percentage: 0 is on code 64 and 100 on code 940. Lines at −10 and 110 are added.
      - mV: 0 is on code 64 and 700 on code 940.
      - HDR: each line is on code 64 plus 876 times the PQ value of its nits. This holds for the dashed lines too.
    - The Vectorscope does not change.
    - `viewer.scopes` with `video_levels` sets it.

## 5. Dual timeline (pp. 66, 643–646, 689–692, 716–718)

**Upper timeline:**
- It shows the whole program at full width, with no zoom.
- Its playhead moves freely. A drag on its ruler, or a sideways two-finger swipe, scrubs the program.
- Most edits work there.
- You can drag clips between the two timelines.
- It shows the timeline In/Out and the markers.

**Lower timeline:**
- It is a detail view around the playhead (p. 644).
- Alt+wheel (Option+wheel on a Mac) and the − and + buttons zoom it. Alt+wheel is the zoom of the Edit page timeline (p. 853).
- Ctrl+wheel (Cmd+wheel on a Mac) moves along it, as on the Edit page timeline (p. 853). Wheel down moves forward.
- On a Mac, a trackpad pinch zooms it too.
- The manual calls its zoom fixed, but Resolve zooms it too.
- A sideways swipe scrolls it (p. 66).
- **Fixed Playhead on (default, D1):**
  - The playhead stays at the center, and the clips move under it.
  - This is also true when you drag or swipe the ruler.
  - Clips do not snap to the playhead (p. 717).
- **Fixed Playhead off:**
  - The clips stay in place, and the playhead moves.
  - The view pages when the playhead reaches an edge.
  - A click on the ruler moves the playhead, and you can drag the playhead.

**Track display:**
- The lower timeline has one row for each video track (p. 646).
  - The label of a video row is the track, over the lowest audio track that holds the linked sound of its clips. An example is "V1" over "A3".
  - When V1 has no linked sound, its label is V1 over the lowest audio track.
  - Another video track with no linked sound has the track alone as its label.
- An audio track has its own row when these three conditions are true:
  - It is above the audio track of the V1 label.
  - It holds a sound clip with no linked picture.
  - It holds no sound clip that is linked to a picture.
- Other audio tracks have no row. The upper timeline shows the same rows.
- A video clip shows a filmstrip. Each picture is the frame under its left edge, and the first picture is the first frame of the clip.
  - Thus a picture that starts before a cut in the source shows the shot before the cut.
- Its audio shows as a waveform band along the bottom. The band becomes larger with the track (p. 647).
  - The band shows one linked sound clip of each picture: the clip on the highest audio track. It shows at its own place. Thus a J or L cut shows past the picture.
  - The band also shows each sound clip with no linked picture on the audio track of the label. A sound clip with no linked picture on another audio track does not show.
  - vidcut: a sound clip with no linked picture in the band takes no clicks.
- Its duration shows as seconds:frames, at the bottom left.
- The clips of a locked track are stippled. The clips of a disabled track are dimmed.
- A disabled clip is dull. A muted clip shows a mute icon.

**Track headers** (pp. 647, 678, 717): Enlarge (one track at a time), Lock, Mute (audio) and Video Enable icons (D43).
- The Mute of a video row shows the state of the audio track of its label.
  - A click sets each audio track that holds the linked sound of its clips to the other state. Other audio tracks do not change.
  - The tooltip is Mute Track or Unmute Track.
  - A video track with no linked sound has a Mute too. A click changes no audio track.
  - vidcut: the click is one undo step. A Mute with no audio track in its label is not lit.
- The row of an audio track has its own Mute. It mutes only that track.
- Each icon has its own column from the left, the same on each track. A track without an icon leaves its column empty.
- A subtitle track has Lock and Enable only, in the Lock and Video Enable columns.
- The labels of the tracks (V1, A1, ST1) are plain text in a column at the right end of the headers. A line separates the column from the icons.
- A Solo icon is between Lock and Mute.
- vidcut has no Solo icon. Its column stays empty, so Mute and Video Enable are in the columns of Resolve.
- vidcut: the name of a track shows between the icons and the label, when the track has a name. A subtitle track always shows its name.

**Automatic tools** (p. 690):
- The **Boring Detector** (Timeline Options) highlights clips on the upper timeline, live as you edit:
  - clips longer than "Boring Clips", in light gray
  - clips shorter than "Jump Cuts", in red
- Analyze Timeline Edits sets both values (D31).
- **Detect Scene Cuts** (Timeline Actions) splits the selected clips where their shots change (D30).

## 6. Track model (pp. 647, 649, 716, 721, 727)

- **Track 1 is the main story (A-roll):**
  - Insert, delete, move and trim on it ripple. Thus it never has gaps.
  - With **Ripple On** off, it behaves like the other tracks.
  - Alt held during a trim turns rippling off for that trim.
- **Tracks 2+ are B-roll:**
  - Each clip is attached to the Track 1 clip under its In point. It follows that clip when Track 1 ripples (p. 649; D21).
  - A trim that moves the Out of the anchor clip to before the In of the attached clip deletes the attached clip (p. 727).
  - These tracks ripple only where there is no Track 1 video under them (p. 721; D22).
  - Clips on these tracks sit freely, with gaps.
- **Audio tracks:**
  - A1, A2, … are below the video tracks. They hold all the sound. A clip on a video track has picture only (pp. 646–647).
  - The sound of a picture on Vn goes on An. A second sound stream goes on the next audio track (D24).
  - Media without video go there by default (D26).
- **Track header** (p. 647):
  - A click makes the track the target (D3).
  - A right-click adds a video or audio track, or removes one (D27).
- **Overlapping video:**
  - The highest enabled track is visible (p. 647).
  - A disabled clip, or a track with its video off, shows what is under it.
- **Linking** (pp. 913–916):
  - A picture clip and its sound clips are linked. They are one link group.
  - An edit of one clip of a group acts on each clip of the group (p. 3875): move, trim, roll, slip, slide, split, join, delete, speed and paste.
  - Each clip of a group moves and trims by the same amount. Thus a J/L cut stays (§8.7).
  - The Cut page shows a picture clip and its linked sound as one item. The sound is the band along the bottom of the item (§5; pp. 646–647).
  - **Link Clips** (pp. 916, 3875): one item in the clip menu, and Ctrl+Alt+L. Any number of picture and sound clips can be in one group.
    - The item needs two or more selected clips. With one clip, it is off, and the context menu does not show it.
    - When the selection is one whole group, the item has a check mark. A click unlinks each clip of the group.
    - Else a click makes the selected clips one new group. Each selected clip leaves its old group.
    - The rest of an old group stays one group when it has two or more clips. A clip that is left alone has no link.
    - vidcut: `clip.unlink` also takes single clips out of their groups.
  - **Linked Selection** (pp. 913–914): a switch on the timeline toolbar, and Ctrl+Shift+L. It is on at first.
    - On, a click on a clip also selects its linked clips, and an edit acts on them all.
    - Off, a clip is selected and edited alone. The clips stay in their group.
    - The switch is the same for each timeline. It is not an undo step.
    - A split with the switch off cuts the clip alone. The part before the cut stays in the group. The part after the cut has no link.
    - vidcut: the switch is in the timeline options. Off, the picture and the sound of an item show apart, each on its own track, as on the Edit page.
    - vidcut: each edit request that acts on clips by id takes `alone`, the same switch for one request.
    - vidcut: the selection holds the clicked clip. With the switch on, the timeline draws its linked clips as selected too.
    - vidcut: off, the linked clips of an edited clip stay where they are. They do not follow Track 1 (D21).
  - **Out of sync** (p. 915): a linked clip that is out of sync shows a red box at the left of its name bar.
    - The box gives a sign, then seconds:frames. 30 frames at 25 fps are 01:05.
    - The picture has the opposite sign of the sound. A later sound gives the picture "-" and the sound "+".
    - A slip gives the same offset as a move of the same frames.
    - The offset counts from the moment of the link. Clips that are apart when a Link joins them are in sync. Linked clips of other media can be out of sync.
    - A sound clip in sync with the picture has no box, also when another sound clip of the group is out of sync. The picture has a box when one of its sound clips is out of sync.
    - vidcut: clips that no Link joined are in sync when their sources play at the same time. An example is a picture and its sound from one edit.
    - vidcut: the picture shows the offset to its first sound clip that is out of sync. A clip with a speed change shows no offset.
  - **The four fixes**: Slip into place, Move into place, Slip others into place and Move others into place, in the menu of the box.
    - "Into place" changes only the clip of the box. "Others into place" changes each other clip of the group that is out of sync with it, each by its own offset.
    - Slip changes the source start and keeps the place. Move changes the place and keeps the source start.
    - A move writes over a clip in the way. That clip keeps its link and stays in sync.
    - On a sound clip, the four fixes are on.
    - On a picture with two or more sound clips, Slip into place and Move into place are off.
    - On a picture with one sound clip, the fix that moves a clip later is off. A later sound turns off Move into place. An earlier sound turns off Move others into place.
    - vidcut: the clip menu of a clip with an offset has the four fixes. `edit.resync` refuses a fix that is off.
  - **Resync Clip** (p. 646, Edit Actions on the Cut page) puts a clip that slipped from the rest of the program back in sync.
    - It does not change a linked clip that has an offset to its group. The four fixes change it.
    - vidcut has no Resync Clip.

## 7. Edit functions (pp. 696–701)

**Workflow** (p. 696):
1. Select a source, and mark its In/Out.
2. Optionally, click the header or number of a track to make it the target.
3. Push an edit button, or press an edit key.
4. Press Q to review the result, and Q again to go back.

Video Only and Audio Only limit what an edit takes (p. 694).

The edit buttons are one row of icons at the bottom of the Media Pool (p. 696). Cut, Dissolve and Smooth Cut are at the right of this row (p. 758). A tooltip gives the name of each icon.

### 7.1 Smart Indicator (p. 696)
- A marker on the ruler shows the edit point nearest the playhead.
- Some functions use this edit point instead of the exact playhead frame.
- After a time without input, the marker bobs.
- Then the edit that it selected flashes green. It flashes red if the clip there has no media past its edge.
- vidcut shows the colors without the motion (deviation X15).

### 7.2 Edit functions
- **Smart Insert** (F9 in the Edit page; p. 697):
  - It inserts at the edit point of the Smart Indicator on the target track, never at any other frame.
  - On Track 1, everything after it moves right by the incoming duration.
- **Append** (Shift+F12; p. 697): it ignores the playhead, and puts the clip after the last clip.
  - Sound alone goes to the audio track after the tracks with the sound of V1. When V1 has no sound, A1 is the track of V1. Thus on an empty timeline, the clip goes to A2.
    - Append makes this track if necessary. The new track has the channels of the clip: a mono clip makes a mono track.
    - vidcut: a track has no channel type ([X153](deviations.md#x153-every-clip-pans-as-on-a-stereo-track)).
    - The clip starts at the end of the timeline. A second Append of sound alone goes on the same track, after the first clip.
  - The sound of a picture does not write over a clip on its audio track:
    - A clip at the first frame of the sound moves the start of the sound to the end of that clip. The sound stops at the next clip. It stays in sync.
    - The append fails, and changes nothing, when the first frame is free and a clip starts inside the sound.
    - The append also fails when no frame of the sound is left.
    - The picture always keeps its full length.
- **Ripple Overwrite** (Shift+F10; p. 698):
  - With no timeline marks, it replaces the whole clip under the playhead.
  - With a timeline In/Out, it replaces exactly that range, possibly across clips.
  - In both cases, everything after it moves by (incoming − replaced) duration. The source duration always wins.
  - The button, the key and a drop treat the other tracks in the same way (D21).
- **Overwrite** (F10; p. 701):
  - It writes over the target track at the playhead, at the timeline In, or backtimed to a lone timeline Out.
  - Other clips do not move.
- **Place On Top** (F12; pp. 699–700):
  - It goes to the first track above all clips at that position, whatever the target track.
  - It makes this track if necessary (D24).
  - Sound alone goes to a new audio track after the last audio track. An empty track does not take it. Then the playhead is at the end of the new clip (D15).
  - It never ripples.
  - It aligns by the first of these rules that applies:
    1. a timeline In with the source In;
    2. a lone timeline Out with the source Out (backtimed);
    3. with no marks, within 5 frames of an edit, with that edit;
    4. otherwise with the playhead.
- **Source Overwrite** (pp. 700–701):
  - It puts a file in sync by timecode with a reference clip on the timeline.
    - The clip's position is the reference's start, plus the file's timecode, minus the timecode of the reference at its start.
    - The timeline's own start timecode plays no part.
    - A file with no timecode tag has timecode 0.
  - A clip counts when the timecode range that it shows overlaps the file. The rest of its media does not count.
    - Audio clips count. Generators do not count.
    - A clip on a disabled track counts, as the reference and for the track.
  - **A compound clip** counts by its own start timecode, as the source and as the reference.
    - The timecode of the clips inside plays no part.
    - Its sound goes on the audio track with the number of the picture's track.
